- **39-4 Gem Rush** (versus, best of 5, mode `gemrush`, ownStep, `act: {label: 'dig'}`): one screen wide, a 34 x 46 grid of 26 px cells that scrolls down (the camera creeps 14 px/s and follows the diggers; anyone left above the top is carried down in the golem's fingers, losing 2 s). Action + a direction digs left, right, down or the lower diagonals (soft 0.18 s, packed 0.45 s, rock never); ▲ jumps one cell; you cannot dig up. Gems 1, big gems 3, the star gem 8 (deep in a rock pocket on the centre line, one entrance each side). Boulders fall when dug under (a shadow line shows where): Buried for 1.5 s and 3 gem points drop. Crumble seams, gas (half-speed digging), water (floods down), geysers, crystal pillars (ring: light gems near them), shovel clash and shove, and the golem's sprinkle (5 gems in the top tunnels at 16 s and 33 s). A round is 50 s, or ends early when the star and 80% of the gems are taken, or when every gem left is above both diggers and nobody has found one for 6 s. Level = 10 s sudden death for a star at the centre (the earth to it is softened so both can reach it). Twists by round: Plain Dig, Boulder Field, Wet Dig (a stream across the middle that carries diggers on foot outward, not while digging or in the air), Lights Low (only lamps and pillar flashes show), Motherlode (a vault of three stars behind a door that opens when both stand on their plates). Gold help (a round behind): a gold shovel (20% faster, Buried 1 s). Deviations from the spec: a loose gem whose cell is dug open tumbles down to the next floor (nothing floats); a digger sealed in by rock for 1.5 s gets the rock beside them cracked to soft earth by the golem ("Crack!"); the timer crystals sit in the golem's hand at the bottom middle so the HUD pills don't cover them. Brain: `MODES.gemrush.plan(st, id, o)` is a Dijkstra over standing spots (walk, side dig, diagonal, jump a step, dig down) scoring gems per second, with a sweep term so it takes the shallow gems before diving (you can't come back up) and a cost on straight shafts and long falls (stairs can be climbed). It commits to each dig, steps off a hole's lip to drop, drifts to the cell's middle in the air, and re-plans if it ends up digging rock. Easy: the nearest gems, the star only late, 0.3-0.5 s between digs. Normal: hesitates up to 0.16 s after a dig, avoids boulder lines and gas, takes a cheap cave-in half the time. Hard: counts the gems it digs through on the way and those near the target, contests the star, always takes a cheap cave-in, re-plans faster, hesitates up to 0.03 s. Lights Low: plans only with what its lamp and pillar flashes showed. Fairness: it sees the other digger 0.2 s late (cpuLag) and digs at a person's speed. Bot results: Normal v Normal 39-31 over 70 matches (sides even within noise; mirrored maps used to make two computers move as exact mirror images, so ties broke one way: pickups now take turns at random and the brains hesitate a random moment); Hard beats Normal 25-7; Normal beats Easy 32-0 (both sides).
- Code: `js/levels/world39/` (the kit `cave-kit.js`, global `CAVE`, reuses World 31's `SHRUNK` helpers and adds `K.panel`, `K.prompter`, Old Mabel `K.mabel`, `K.ofx` (soot, crook, dust, slime, dirt, sack, hatDown, crown), `K.back` cave backdrops, `K.crystal`, `K.cluster`, `K.rock`, `K.stalactites`, `K.glowWorms`, `K.mushrooms`, `K.gem`, `K.golemFace`, `K.golemHand`, `K.wormHead`, `K.wormSeg`, the four outfits, shared `cave*` sounds, the songs `cavedeep`, `cavegolem0-3`, `cavechase`, `cavechase1-3`, `cavechaseLow`, `cavedig`, `cavedigFast` and the ambiences `caveShaft`, `caveCavern`, `caveRumble`, `caveChamber`; its header is the manual), brains in `js/cpu/levels/world39/`, previews in `js/previews/world39.js` (helpers `pw39*`), tools in `tools/w39/` (`check.js`, `trace.js`, `shots.js`). `world.js` pushes the four defs; World 39's script tags sit in one block. Each team level has a `plan(st)` that the prompts, the HUD chips and the brain all read. Built in parallel with World 38 (no World 38 file touched).
- Review (Fable, Nov 2026): matches the spec; medals, music, controls, previews, ready cards and outfits all check out. Fixed: 39-1 the computer pair took a Snag or a Lurch at a shelf on every seed (the plan's tilt sat one degree from the Lurch, and a hero walked off to a job mid-tilt): the plan now tilts to 3 degrees under the Lurch, the brain leaves jobs (not bats, urgent sparks or the crate) until past the shelf and keeps tighter to the plan's tilt there, and the shelf prompts speak up sooner (pair now 167 s, no Lurch or Snag, 20/20 gems). 39-2 the forearm lift scooped the soother off the rib ledge every cycle (heroes on a body ledge are no longer picked up); a soft lock where a person holding ▲ ran straight up the next stacked climb and could never stop on the knee (`climbLock`: a held key does not start the next climb until released); the plan's `noJump` (no hop within 0.85 s of a sweep) and `flee` (leave the palm before the fist); the brain holds on with action + ▼; Rage tuned a little kinder (pull 0.1, flinch 0.07). 39-3 only the lurer's bandolier, which was drawn across the face. 39-4 the gold shovel for the trailing digger made them 25% SLOWER (it divided by the bonus), which turned rounds into 3-0 sweeps; the fifth sprinkle gem always fell on Pip's half; a boulder now breaks up on the digger it buries and unsupported boulders always fall; the geyser only bumped heads on its own hollow, so it now blasts a chimney up to 6 cells and throws the digger up it. After: computer v computer is even (28-32 over 60 matches), Hard beats Normal about 9 in 10.
- Tools: `tools/w39/` `check.js`, `trace.js`, `shots.js`, `stuck.js` (person-like partners: lag, eager, slow) and `versus.js` (Gem Rush computer v computer).
- Left as is: 39-3 Gold is 175 s and the computer pair always runs about 168 s (thin); a Tantrum (39-2) never happens with a computer partner.
- Status: built, bot-tested and reviewed (Nov 2026); not yet played by hand.

### World 40: Colossus March (boss)
The boss of worlds 31 to 40, one long level **40-1 The Iron Colossus** (mode `colossus`, team, 8 parts with a checkpoint at the start of each, built like 30-1 and 35-1). Gavyn picked it on 2026-11-14 and asked for "a hyper stylized kind of hand drawn look ... very high quality and multiple rounds of redrawing"; he approved the Round 0 proof as the bar ("Everything else should be at least this quality"). The full design is the spec at `pip-and-bo/notes/world40-iron-colossus-spec.md` (project files); the header of `js/levels/world40/iron-colossus.js` and of each part file hold the rules as built.
- **Story:** a mountain at the edge of Pyramid Sands stands up: a walking iron giant marching on Sunny Meadow, driven by TICK, a tiny wind-up tin toy. Pip and Bo chase its feet, climb it, trick its fists into breaking its chest locks, go inside to pull its three power cores, get out while the drawing is rubbed out, and settle who is champ with a Thumb War on its two clasped hands. Tick ends as a friend.
- **Parts:** 1 Under the Feet (scrolling right: footfalls with growing shadows, wave hops, the heel chain tow), 2 The Swinging Leg (up a leg that tilts each stride; greased plates only runnable mid-swing), 3 The Fists (the lantern eye hunts whoever taunted; dodge so the fist hits the lock), 4 The Boiler (hold a valve while the other dashes through steam; pressure), 5 The Gear Heart (signal-booth routes for a carriage on the heart's tick), 6 The Lantern Head (lightning quarters, rubber mats, the bulb), 7 Coming Apart (an eraser edge sweeps in; launch plates with a pump, chain bridges, crumbling platforms, steam-lift walls; Tick rides a hero's head), 8 Thumb War (versus, best of 3, inside the team level with the clock stopped).
- **Fails:** nobody is hurt. Every fail is a counted "Clang!" and a pop back to a safe spot. Rivets are the collectibles (10, 10, 8, 8, 8, 6, 10, 0).
- **Thumb War (Part 8):** each drives a giant thumb. Action rears and strikes (a knock), ▲ jukes, ▼ tucks, ◀ ▶ lean in or back. Both striking is a clonk; a strike on a juke, tuck or out of reach whiffs and leaves you flat; a strike on a flat or cramped thumb (or one with an empty stamina bar) is a PIN. Pinned: three taps on the flag's beats escape, an off-beat tap resets the count. Rounds have a 40 s cap then a slam-off; the loser of a round gets the thimble (double knocks for 10 s). Computer opponent: sees late (Easy 0.45 s, Normal 0.28 s, Hard 0.14 s), jukes or tucks wind-ups, punishes open thumbs. Bot results: no side bias; Hard beats Normal about 4 in 5, Normal beats Easy every time, a slow kid beats Easy about 3 in 4.
- **Medals** (play time, Part 8 and idle waits not counted, plus team Clangs): Gold 12:00 with 12 or fewer Clangs, Silver 18:00 with 30 or fewer. Bot runs: the computer pair takes 528 s with 0 Clangs (chase 81, leg 54, fists 82, boiler 75, heart 90, brain 108, fall 38); noisy pairs about 742 s with about 32 Clangs.
- **Outfits** (`iron-outfits.js`): `ironchase` (Part 1: Pip brass goggles and a streaming cream scarf that snaps straight on the chain tow; Bo a white aviator cap and green neckerchief; both get sand-dusted after a ground wave), `ironrig` (Parts 2-3: Pip a dented yellow hard hat and green tool belt with a swinging wrench; Bo a teal welder's mask tipped up above the ears, cream gloves, a silver hip chain), `ironcrew` (Parts 4-8: Pip a head torch and cream bib, Bo brass ear-defenders, grey bib and yellow rag). Soot builds on the bibs part by part, hats sit crooked for 3 s after a Clang, and each Thumb War round win hangs a gold bolt on a ribbon.
- **Music** (`iron-music.js`, registered there so `music-tracks.js` is untouched): one tune runs through everything, Tick's music-box melody. `ironmarch` / `ironmarch2` / `ironmarch3` (Parts 1-3, a slow minor march stepping up a key each part), `ironworks` (Parts 4-6, clockwork), `ironfall` (Part 7, fast and bright), `ironthumb` / `ironthumbFast` (Part 8 jig, fast for the last 10 s and the slam-off). Ambiences `ironDesert`, `ironHeights`, `ironCity`, `ironBelly`, `ironMeadow`. All pass `tools/audio/check.js` at -22 LUFS.
- **Preview** (`js/previews/world40.js`, helpers `pw40`): 6 s in the sketch style: the whole Colossus strides and a foot lands (THOOM) just behind the running heroes, a fist KLANGs a chest lock, the two lift a glowing core, and the giant fades back to pencil. Still frame at 1.0 s.
- **Code:** `js/levels/world40/` (`sketch-kit.js` the drawing kit, `iron-art*.js` sheets per part, `iron-colossus.js` the core, `iron-<part>.js` one file per part, `iron-hud.js`, `iron-outfits.js`, `iron-music.js`), brains in `js/cpu/levels/world40/`. Tools: `tools/w40/play.js <part|all> --who=pair|noisy|kid|idle` (bot runs), `tools/w40/shot.js <part> secs out.png [--crop] [--eval]`.
- **Checks:** every team part clears with the computer pair (0 Clangs, every rivet) and with noisy and kid partners on either side; none clears with one player idle.
- **Sketch kit rules learned in review:** (1) every solid form in a sheet starts with `d.solid(points)`: it lays opaque paper and rubs out whatever was drawn under it in that sheet, so later forms hide earlier ink (without it, backdrops and earlier forms show through). (2) Sheets are one flat canvas per boil version; pass `layers: true` only for a sheet that is blitted with `undo` (only the preview's giant does). The working layers are freed as soon as a sheet is made and `SKETCH.forget` frees a part's sheets at once: a part holds 30 to 85 MB of drawings (it was 200 to 560 MB). (3) Keep big shapes inside their sheet and put a drawn clamp or seam over every sheet join.
- **Lifts instead of hand-ups (2026-11-15, Gavyn):** the walls in Parts 1 and 7 have a STEAM LIFT (a piston plate at the foot with a lever on the ground): it rises only while someone holds the action at one of its two levers (one at the foot, one on top) and sinks when nobody does: one rides the plate while the other holds the low lever, then the one on top holds the top lever to lift the other. On The Swinging Leg the hero up top pulls a clamp lever that locks the ballast block, so the one below climbs it. `ironHandUp`, `ironHauled` and the crouch boost are gone; the computer partner uses the shared `ironBrainLift`.
- **Review (2026-11-15):** Parts 2, 3, 7 and 8 were redrawn to the proof's standard (solid leg parts; whole pauldrons, rib clamps and a drawn eye beam on the chest wall; pencil-and-rivet platforms, body and arm slide in Part 7; detailed hands in Part 8); the preview lost a grey box behind the giant. Checked: bot pair, noisy and kid runs, idle runs, medals, audio, controls, checkpoints (resume after a reload, restart this part, start over), outfits, preview. Still plain: the Superhero City towers and Tumbleweed Gulch backdrops, the hill cottage in Part 7, and the hint bubbles, which use the game's normal font. At phone size the top status pills overlap the title banner a little. Slowest kid-bot runs: Part 2 about 590 s, Part 4 about 55 Clangs with Bo as the kid.
- **Part 3 fix (Gavyn's playtest, 2026-11-15):** the spotlight was hard to steer and the goal unclear. Now a taunt LOCKS the eye on you (your partner walking about no longer steals it) and it only narrows on someone who taunted; it moves faster (560 px/s, was 230), narrows sooner (0.8 s within 40 px, was 1.0 s within 26 px) and a rust ring closes round the pool as it locks on; standing anywhere on the lock (74 px) snaps the slam onto it. A bobbing yellow arrow names the jobs ("PIP: TAUNT HERE!" over the lock, "BO: HOLD HERE TO PULL!" over the elbow), a banner states the goal at the start, Tick's first line hints at it and the ready card spells it out. Bots: pair 75 s (was 82), kid partners about 260-370 s (was 280-560).
- Status: built, bot-tested and reviewed (Nov 2026); Part 3 reworked after Gavyn's first playtest.

## How the game is built

### Graphics polish (Nov 2026)
Gavyn (2026-11-16): refine and add detail to every level's graphics (not World 32 or 40), every word inside its box, a professional look. Style guide and batch plan: project file `pip-and-bo/notes/graphics-polish/style-guide.md`.
- **Shared art** (`js/render.js`): sky with sun rays and shaded clouds, three layers of hills with bushes, carved stone with grass blades and flowers, planks with grain and brackets, spikes, water, sand, buttons, flags, the door, bridges, HUD pills and the intro card. Worlds 32 and 40 keep the plain versions (`artPlain()`, `ART_PLAIN_WORLDS`).
- **Speed**: drawing all of this live made frames about twice as slow on a computer without a graphics chip (a slow phone draws the canvas the same way). So it is drawn into pictures and copied: the sky and hills once per level and theme (`bgLayers`; one flattened picture while the camera doesn't move), clouds once per size, and the level's stone in chunks of 16 columns (`drawStoneChunks`, rebuilt only when a chunk's tiles change; `drawStone` alone, used by levels that draw their own tiles, copies one small picture per tile). Copies land on whole screen pixels with smoothing off: smoothing made each copy several times slower. Measure with `node tools/polish/perf.js <repo folder> <levels>` (render time with the canvas flush, and live frames a second, on a CPU slowed 4x) against a checkout of main: frame cost is now within noise of main.
- Helpers for levels: `mixCol(a, b, t)`, `shade(c, t)`, `tint(c, t)` ('#rrggbb' colours), `artPlain()`.
- The intro card grows to its widest line, and name tags to their words.
- **Checking**: `node tools/polish/survey.js [w1-5|codes|all] [--sizes=laptop,phone]` saves ready / play / pause screenshots on laptop and phone sizes to `build/polish/` and reports text that spills out of its box or off screen (canvas and menus); `node tools/polish/summary.js build/polish/report-*.json` lists the findings.

### Medals (js/medals.js)

- A team level gives its goals once, as `medals` on its `MODES` object (or on its `LEVELS` entry when it has no mode):
  `medals: { bronze: 'Reach the castle', goals: [{ key: 'time', gold: 210, silver: 330 }, { key: 'boings', gold: 3, silver: 10, word: 'Boing' }] }`.
- Each goal names a value by `key`. Fewer (or faster) is better unless `more: true`. Leave out `silver` (or `gold`) when that medal doesn't ask for it. Gold needs every gold goal, Silver every silver goal, Bronze only the finish (the `bronze` words). `pct: true` shows a 0-1 fraction as a percent; `word` names the thing counted ("Boing" becomes "3 or fewer Boings"; "the stars" with `pct` becomes "At least 50% of the stars"); `text: n => '...'` replaces the words.
- Values: `time` (G.time at the clear) and `falls` are built in. Everything else comes from the mode's `medalValues()` hook at the clear, e.g. `medalValues() { return { boings: 5, stars: 0.6 }; }` (it may return `time` too, or `{ noMedal: true }` for a finish that earns nothing).
- The medal is worked out by js/medals.js only. A level's `stats()` must not compute or print its own medal any more (no "Medal" tile, no "Silver medal · " line). Results keep `rec.medal = { tier: 0-3, vals }`; the best tier is saved as `medal:<level name>`.
- Where it shows: the ready card lists the goals and your best medal; the results card has the medal title, the three medals beside it and the ladder; the world map has a running tally (levels with Gold / Silver / Bronze out of all team levels, a Gold counting for all three) and each world tile shows a little medal per team level in its best medal (an empty outline until one is earned) and blue crossed swords for every versus level (always the same, Gavyn, Nov 2026); each level card on a world page shows its best medal in the corner. Tests: `node tools/medals/check.js` (every team level), and `node tools/cpu-test.js` prints the medal a computer pair earns.
- Worlds 1-8 had no medals before (Nov 2026); their goals were set from computer-pair times (Gold about 1.6-3x a perfect pair's time, Silver about 3-6x) plus the level's own mistakes or stars: falls (World 1), squashes (block building), Robo's points (Robo Pong, a loss earns no medal), strokes over par (golf), cones, stars, ouches, smooth trips, gems or gulps.
- Older level notes below that mention a "Medal" or "Trophy" tile, or a level's own medal function, describe the card before js/medals.js; the same thresholds now live in each mode's `medals`.
- Medal goals should be reachable: Bronze for any finish, Silver for a decent run, Gold for a clean, quick one (check with the computer pair: tools/cpu-test.js).

### Conventions for every level
- Fixed physics step `DT` = 1/120 s; canvas 960×540 with 30 px tiles (32×18). Maps are 18 rows of 32 characters with spawns `1` and `2`.
- Versus: `kind: 'versus'`, `match: { bestOf, roundTime }`. End a round with `matchRoundEnd(winner, reason)` (`-1` is a tie). Reasons are short, end in "!", and name who fell when it matters.
- Team: `kind: 'team'`, timed, ends at the door or with `levelClear()`. Falls are counted unless `noFalls: true`.
- Action: `def.act = { label }` (lowercase, 6 letters or fewer). `act.labels = [pip, bo]` gives each role its own word; `act.padGlyph` names another controller button. Modes read `p.pendingAct` (a fresh press) or `p.input.act` (held), never the keyboard directly.
- A level with rooms can name them: `roomWord: 'Race'` makes the shared HUD pill and intro card say "Race 2 of 4" instead of "Room 2 of 4" (11-2 does).
- Every level has a short `tip` (about 100 characters; rooms can have their own). The ready card and the intro card show it; the pause card shows the current room's tip.
- Controls text: write a level's controls once, as keyboard lines in `def.legend` (`{ pip, bo }` whole lines with `<kbd>` keys, or `{ jump, down }` to rename those two words). The game rewrites them for whatever each player holds. Do not write a separate controller or touch line (`legend.pad` in older levels is no longer shown). A level that introduces a new mechanic adds `howTo: ['...', '...']` (2-4 short lines) for the ready card.
- Button prompts in a level: use `btnLabel(id, what)` for text and `drawBtn(id, what, x, y, { label })` to draw the button (`what`: `left`, `right`, `jump`, `down`, `act`, `move`). Never print `F`, `A` or `▲` by hand, and never read `Pads.kind` (it is global, the last device anyone touched).
- Results card: `stats()` returns about 4 tiles and 1-2 lines. A team level scored by something other than time sets `noTime: true` (no time record or Time tile).
- No leaks between levels: level state lives in `G.mst` (fresh on every `loadLevel`), and match levels reset per-round state in `resetRound`. Prefix every top-level name and `Sfx.extra` sound with the level's mode name.
- Mode map characters: `parseLevel` takes `1 2 a b c E k F * G T H J R L M O Q U V @` (and `w s X ^ = o u #`, plus any letter in `switches`) before a mode sees the grid, so pick other characters for your own markers (9-4 lost its first dock plank to `k`, the key).
- Per-role action words: `act.labels` is per player, so a level whose roles swap every room gives each room its own `act` (as 8-3 does) or updates the labels itself (9-4 rewrites the `[data-actlabel]` text when the room changes).
- Never rename a shipped level: records are saved in localStorage by level name (`pipbo:best:<name>`, `pipbo:golf:<name>`). A level whose course changes so much that old bests can't be compared sets `recordName` (records only; medals and "played" stay under the name), as 3-4 Portal Park did for its long course.
- Catch-up help is gold and announced, never hidden.
- Every new level declares its outfit (`def.outfit`, or the world's `WORLDS[n].outfit`; see "Outfits").

### Code map
The code is split into `css/` and `js/` files (the README has the same table). `index.html` loads the scripts in this order.

| File | What's in it |
|---|---|
| `index.html` | Markup: `#stage` with `canvas#game`, touch pads `#padPip` and `#padBo`, `#overlay` cards, `#toast`; script tags in load order |
| `css/style.css` | All styles |
| `js/core.js` | Constants (`T`, `DT`, `PHYS`), colours `C`, themes and `applyTheme`, helpers |
| `js/outfits.js` | Outfits: `defineOutfit`, `defineOutfitPart`, the parts library (hats, clothes, capes, goggles...), `setOutfit`, `outfitNow`, `drawOutfit` / `outfitLayer` (drawPlayer and the previews' `v.hero` call it) |
| `js/outfits-worlds.js` | The outfits of Worlds 1-31 and which level wears which (`OUTFIT_BY_LEVEL`, `OUTFIT_BY_WORLD`, `TTH_OUTFITS`) |
| `js/levels/level-list.js` | `LEVELS` (with a `/* ===== World N (` marker per world from 4 on) and `WORLDS` (`name`, `blurb`, `theme`) |
| `js/saving.js` | `store`, `levelRecord` |
| `js/audio.js` | `Sfx`: the mixer (music and sounds volume, reverb), `tone`, `noise`, the shared sounds and the texture layers (each level keeps its `Sfx.extra.*` sounds in its own file) |
| `js/music.js` | `Music`: the song sequencer, the instruments (`MUSIC_INST`) and drums (`MUSIC_DRUMS`), the ambience player; `Music.tick()` runs every frame |
| `js/music-tracks.js` | The songs (`SONGS`), the ambience beds (`AMBIENCE`), and which world or level plays which (`MUSIC_BY_WORLD`, `AMB_BY_WORLD`, `AMB_BY_LEVEL`) |
| `js/player.js` | `makePlayer`, tile collision (`solidFor`), movement, tether rope/chain/bungee, stomp, `kill`/`respawn` |
| `js/levels/block-building.js`, `golf.js`, `robo-pong.js` | World 2-3 mechanics (`BUILD`, `GOLF`, `PONG`) and their drawing |
| `js/versus.js` | `MODES` registry, `hook`/`drawHook`, the best-of-N framework (`MATCH`, `matchRoundEnd`), `knock` |
| `js/levels/world4/` to `world33/` | One file per level: its `MODES.<name>` block, its own drawing and `Sfx.extra` sounds, e.g. `world7/bumper-cars.js` |
| `js/input.js` | Keyboard (`keys`, `BIND`), controllers (`Pads`, `readPad`), touch, `pollInput` |
| `js/controls.js` | Each player's device (`playerDevice`), button prompts (`btnLabel`, `btnHTML`, `drawBtn`, `controlsLine`), the ready card (`startLevel`, `showReadyCard`, `readyPress`) |
| `js/ui.js` | Overlay cards (`pause`/`showPauseCard`, `showResult`, `showWorldDone`, `showFinal`, `armOverlay`, `menuBack`), legend (`setLegend`, `setActUI`), toast, mute and volume (`sysCfgHTML`, `setVolume`) |
| `js/render.js` | Shared drawing (`render`, `drawHUD`, `drawIntro`) and particles |
| `js/previews/preview-core.js` | Level previews for the world pages: `definePreview`, the drawing kit `pvKit`, the plain fallback, the title banner, and the loop that animates every `canvas[data-pv]` in a menu |
| `js/previews/worldN.js` | One file per world: a `definePreview('<level name>', ...)` per level |
| `js/home.js` | Home screens: `showTitle`, `showWorlds` (world map), `showWorld` (a world's page) |
| `js/main.js` | `G`, `parseLevel`, `roomDef`, `loadLevel`, `step`, `nextRoom`, `levelClear`, `frame`, `fit`, `window.__pipbo`, startup (loads last) |

- There is no wrapper function: all files share globals, so a file may use at load time only what the files above it define.
- New level: a new `js/levels/worldN/<slug>.js` with a `MODES.<name>` block (hooks: `setup` required; `resetRound`, `go`, `pre`, `step`, `draw`, `drawTop`, `hud`, `stats`, `timeUp`, `tiebreak`, `onKill`, `onRespawn`, `onStomp`, `room` optional), its def in `LEVELS` after its world's marker (`name`, `world` 0-based, `kind`, `mode`, `tip`, `map` or `rooms`), and a script tag after the last level file, before `js/input.js`. Level codes follow the order in `LEVELS`.
- New world: a `WORLDS` entry with a theme plus a marker at the end of `LEVELS`. A world with no levels stays hidden. Add `js/previews/worldN.js` and its script tag after the other preview files (before `js/home.js`).
- Every new level also gets a preview (see "Home screens" below). Until it has one, its card shows a plain scene in its world's colours.

### Controls and the ready card (Nov 2026)
Gavyn asked for cleaner controls: the game should see what each player is using and show matching prompts, levels should have a screen you can read before they start, and with two players both should ready up.
- **Detection** (`js/input.js`, `js/controls.js`): `Dev.last[k]` is the device player k last pressed (`kb`, `pad`, `touch`). `playerDevice(k)` returns `{ kind, family, name }`; before a first press it is the controller assigned to them, else touch on a touch screen, else the keyboard. A controller's family comes from its id (`xbox`, `ps`, `nintendo`), and a pad the browser can't map is `generic` (buttons shown as 1, 2, 3). In 1-player mode both sets of keys are the person's, and prompts follow the set they last used (`Dev.keyset`).
- **Prompts**: `btnLabel` / `btnHTML` / `drawBtn` give one button for one player. `controlsLine(k, html)` rewrites a keyboard line (`<kbd>A</kbd><kbd>D</kbd> move <kbd>W</kbd> jump`) into that player's buttons: A becomes the controller's jump button, the arrows become `stick ◀ ▶`, touch shows ◀ ▶ ▲ ▼ and ●. `glyph(k, id)` names a controller button for a player; older level code that calls `actKey(id)` then `glyph(k)` still gets that player's own controller.
- **Top-bar legend** (`legendRender`): one line per person in their own buttons, built from `def.legend` and the action word. It hides for a touch player (their buttons are on screen) and for the computer's character. Levels still set it with `setLegend(lg)`.
- **Ready card** (`showReadyCard`): the menus start a level with `startLevel(i)` (level cards, "Play World", "Next level", "On to World N"). The card shows the world, level code, Team / Versus, best-of, the tip, optional `howTo` lines, and a box per player with their device, their controls and a Ready button. A person readies with their own jump or action button (W / F / Space for Pip, ↑ / / / Enter for Bo, A / X / Start on their own controller, or a tap on their box); one player cannot ready the other. The level starts 0.6 s after the last person is ready. In 1-player mode the computer's box is already ready. Esc, B or Back returns to the world's page.
- After the card, a level without rooms skips the old intro card and only shows the name tags for about a second (`G.introQuick`); a level with rooms keeps its "Room 1 of 3" card. `loadLevel(i)` itself still starts at once, so R, "Restart level", "Play it again", rematches and the test tools are unchanged.
- The pause card lists each person's controls the same way (`controlsLinesHTML`).
- **Controls as a vertical table (Gavyn, 2026-11-15)**: the ready card, the pause card and How to play list controls one action a row, key or button on the left, what it does on the right (`controlRows` / `controlsTableHTML` in `js/controls.js`, CSS `.ctl-table`). Pip and Bo sit side by side (stacked under 520px wide); the pause card widens to 820px for two. Rows are cut from the same `def.legend` keyboard line: each `<kbd>` group starts a row and the words after it are its label, words before the first key join the first row, a line with no keys becomes a small note, and the action button is the last row. So a legend should read as "keys label keys label" (e.g. `<kbd>W</kbd> climb <kbd>S</kbd> dive`); don't put one row's label before its keys.
- **Settings (Gavyn, 2026-11-14)** (`js/settings.js`): one screen, opened by Settings on the title and on the pause card (Back / Esc / B returns to whichever opened it). It holds Music and Sounds volume, Sound on/off and Full screen (`sysCfgHTML` in js/ui.js; the title no longer has loose sound buttons), and each player's own keys (move left, move right, jump, drop, action) and controller buttons (jump, action). Pick a button, then press the new key or controller button; a key the other player had swaps over; Esc / Start cancels. Reserved: Esc, Tab, R, M, Ctrl / Alt; the d-pad, Start, View and Home. Menus always keep arrows / Enter / Esc and stick / A / B. Keys rewrite `BIND` in place and `KEYCAPS` follows (so `btnLabel`, `btnHTML`, `controlsLine` and How to play show the new keys); controller choices live in `PadBind` and `readPads` applies them, so `Pads.prev[ix].jump` and `glyph('a')` follow too. Saved as `keyBind` and `padBind`. Test: `node tools/settings/check.js`.
- **Every future level:** write keys only through `def.legend`, `btnLabel` / `btnHTML` / `drawBtn` or `glyph(..)`; never print a fixed key letter or read a key code (`keys.has('KeyW')`) by hand, or Settings can't change it. A few older levels still print fixed key letters in their own hints (e.g. 5-1 Two-Seat Car's "W go · S brake").
- Changed by hand: 13 older levels (Worlds 2-8: block building, golf, Robo Pong and others) had their own "is this a controller?" check with the global `Pads.kind`; they now ask `actKey(id)`. Their hint wording is unchanged.

### Music and sound (Nov 2026)
Gavyn (2026-11-14): the game needs a base background music, themed music where it fits, and richer sound effects, with each level having its own sounds where it makes sense. Everything is made live with WebAudio, so the game stays self-contained: no sound files.

- **Music.** `SONGS` in `js/music-tracks.js`, written as short text patterns (the format is explained at the top of `js/music.js`): `menu` (title and menus), `base` (World 1, and the fallback for a world with no song yet) and one song per world. `MUSIC_BY_WORLD` maps a world index to a song; a level or room can pick its own with `music: 'name'` in its def, and a mode can switch part-way with `Music.cue({ song, amb })` (a boss phase, a new era). Versus levels play their song 6% faster. The song turns down on the ready card and the pause card.
- **Ambience.** A quiet bed under a level (wind, waves, crickets, a crowd, a factory hum...) with small random events (a bird, a drip, thunder): `AMBIENCE`, mapped by `AMB_BY_WORLD` and `AMB_BY_LEVEL`, or `amb: 'name'` in a level's def (`amb: null` for none). It plays through the Sounds volume.
- **Sound effects.** Levels still write their own sounds as `Sfx.extra.<mode><Word>` with `this.tone(...)`, and can now use `this.noise(dur, { type, f, to, q, vol, a, delay })` for anything a beep cannot do. **Name a sound for what it is**: `Sfx.play` adds a texture layer picked from the words in the name after the mode prefix (`pyrCrash` gets a heavy thud, `sbSplash` water, `vikGold` a sparkle; the word lists are `Sfx.texKinds` in `js/audio.js`). `Sfx.texture.off['name'] = true` switches it off for one sound.
- **Volume.** Music and Sounds each have Off, Low, Medium, High (pause card: a row each; title screen: one button each that steps through), saved as `volMusic` and `volSfx`. M and "Sound on/off" still mute everything. Sound effects are mixed a little above the music; every song is balanced to the same loudness with its `vol`.
- **Every new world** gets: its own song (or a fitting existing one) in `MUSIC_BY_WORLD`, an ambience per world or level, and sounds named in plain words so the textures apply. A new song needs its `vol` set: `node tools/audio/check.js` prints the value that brings it level with the others (run it twice; it fails a song more than 0.8 LUFS off the target). A new world gets a new song rather than reusing one.
- **Tools.** `node tools/audio/check.js` checks that every song is well-formed and renders, measures loudness, checks that every level resolves to a song and ambience, plays every sound effect, and checks that the music follows the menus, levels, pause and volume buttons (`--list` prints every level's song and ambience, `--wav` saves each song to `build/audio/`). `node tools/audio/jukebox.js` builds `build/sound-test.html`, a page to listen to every song, ambience and texture.
- **Status (2026-11-14):** 38 songs: every world has its own (Gavyn: no one song on half the levels; a few levels sharing one is fine). The levels of a world share its song but each plays it in its own key (`tr` in `Music.pick`), and versus levels a little faster. 30-1 plays each era's song through `MODES.timethief.music()` (a mode may define `music()` returning `{ song, amb }`). Gavyn heard the first 15 and said they all sound good, but some were a little louder or quieter than others: songs are now balanced by perceived loudness (LUFS, target -22), not plain average level. Not done: a pass over levels with few sounds of their own (Worlds 27 and 28 have the fewest).

### Outfits (Nov 2026)
Gavyn asked for Pip and Bo to wear outfits unique to each level where it fits, and for every future level to get them while it is built.
- **What an outfit is**: paint over the usual body, drawn by `drawPlayer` (js/render.js) in four layers: `back` (capes, packs, tanks: behind the body), `body` (clothes and masks: on the body, under the eyes), `head` (hats), `front` (goggles, visors, a clown nose). Hitboxes, physics, the computer partner and saves never see it. A hat can lift Pip's sprout so it grows out of the hat's top (`lift`), and a hood can put Bo's ears over itself (`earsOver`).
- **Declaring one** (the first found wins): `setOutfit('name')` from a level's own code at run time (lasts until the next `loadLevel`; the Time Thief changes outfit every era with it), `rooms[i].outfit`, the level's `def.outfit`, `WORLDS[w].outfit`. `'none'` turns them off for a level. Worlds 1-31 declare theirs by level name in `js/outfits-worlds.js` (so `level-list.js` stayed as it was); a new world puts `outfit: 'name'` on its level defs and/or `WORLDS[n].outfit` in its own `world.js`, and `defineOutfit` calls in its kit or world file.
- **Defining one**: `defineOutfit('pirates', { pip: [['tricorn', { feather: '#e8483a' }], ['sash', {}]], bo: [['bandana', {}], ['shirt', { stripes: '#2f5fc4' }]] })` (or `both: [...]`). Each entry is a part from the library in `js/outfits.js` with its options; colour options take a colour or `'own'` / `'ownDark'` / `'ownLight'` (the wearer's colours). `{ from: 'pirates', noHats: true }` copies an outfit without its hats, for a level that already puts job hats on (Runaway Ship's captain, Train Tracks' builder). A level can add its own part with `defineOutfitPart('name', { layer, lift, earsOver, draw(c, o, k) })`: it draws in the body's own frame (x -11 to 11, y -26 at the top of the head to 0 at the feet, eyes about y -16), scaled to the body as drawn; the header of `js/outfits.js` lists what `c` holds.
- **Rules**: Pip must still read as orange and Bo as blue at a glance: keep most of the body in their own colour, Pip wears no blue (navy counts) and Bo wears no orange or red (the Fable review recoloured about 25 outfits for this; purple, green, yellow, teal, white and dark ink are safe for both; a clown's red nose is the one exception). A level that puts its own prop on the head (Sprinkle Catch's baskets) gets an outfit with no hat; Pip and Bo never wear the same hat (a different hat, or the same hat in different colours); keep the eyes visible; keep it small and bold (the bodies are 22 x 26 px on screen).
- **Heroes a level draws itself** (vehicles, close-ups, custom poses): call `outfitBehind(id, x, y, w, h, o)` before the body and `outfitOver(...)` after the face, or `drawOutfit(id, layer, ...)` per layer, where (x, y) is the middle of the feet and w x h the body in the current transform; `outfitSproutLift(id)` says how far up to draw Pip's sprout (17-4 Vine Swing and 23-4 Pie-Eating Contest do this). Levels that call `drawPlayer` (most do, even for copies in vehicles) get outfits for free.
- **Previews**: `v.hero()` wears the previewed level's outfit by itself (`o.outfit` forces one, `false` none); the title screen's run through the worlds shows each world's look (`OUTFIT_BY_WORLD`).
- **Levels left as they were**: World 1 (their everyday look, so new players meet the real Pip and Bo); Worlds 32-35 and the custom-drawn versus levels that were already dressed for the job when outfits came (superhero suits, storm-chaser raincoats, Viking helmets, wizard robes; and 13-4 Broomstick Race, 14-4 Raptor Race, 15-4 Planet Hop Race, 18-2 Grapple & Zip, 18-4 Plank Duel, 19-4 Quick-Draw Showdown, 21-4 Paper Plane Race, 22-4 Dolphin Race), and the levels where Pip and Bo are a ball, a chip, a paddle or a car. Outfits there would double up the hats.
- **Checking**: `node tools/outfits/sheet.js [names|all]` draws every outfit big (both players, facing both ways, standing and running, plus the preview look) into `build/outfits/`. Then look at the level itself and its preview.

### Home screens (Nov 2026)

**Menu colours (Gavyn, 2026-11-14):** Gavyn disliked the purplish (dusk-indigo) menus and picked **Charcoal** from four mockups (Midnight Navy, Deep Teal, Charcoal, Warm Cream). Every menu colour is a variable in `:root` at the top of `css/style.css` (`--cabinet`, `--cabinet-2`, `--line`, `--paper`, `--muted`, the `--*-rgb` triples for see-through fills, `--card-top`, `--tile-top`, `--boss-top`...), so a new scheme is a change to that block only: don't hard-code menu colours elsewhere, and keep purple out of menus and HUD. The shared canvas HUD (pills, banners, countdown in `js/render.js`, the preview pill) uses the same charcoal `rgba(23,25,29,a)`. The title backdrop swaps the stock blue-violet stone for slate (`pvDrawHero`). Not changed: `C.ink` and the `rgba(28,26,54,a)` ink inside level art and each level's own HUD panels (about 150 files).

Gavyn asked for a new title and home screen where every world can be picked and each world shows little animated scenes of its levels.
- **Title** (`showTitle`, restyled Nov 2026 when Gavyn asked for a more professional look): a full-screen title like a console game's. The backdrop canvas (`data-pv="hero"`, `pvDrawHero` in preview-core.js) fills the whole title box: it is always 480 wide and as tall as the box's shape needs (`heroH`), with Pip and Bo running through every world in turn. Over it: the logo (one span per letter, so the letters drop in and bob, a dark outline, a stacked drop shadow for depth and a lighter top half from `::after`), the tagline "A couch co-op adventure for two", and a frosted menu panel with Play, the Players setting (`cpuCfgHTML`) and How to play, plus "Press Enter / A to start". Small pills in the bottom-left corner count worlds, levels and head-to-heads. On short screens (a phone held sideways) the logo sits on the left and the menu on the right. Play opens the world map at the world played last (`store` key `lastWorld`). All title animation stops for people who prefer reduced motion. Check: `node tools/title/title.js` (layout at 4 screen sizes in 1 and 2 player modes, plus keyboard, controller and touch).
- **How to play** (`showHowTo`): each player's keys, the touch buttons, the controller, and the one-line pitch (`titleLede`). These used to sit on the title. Back (Esc / B) returns to the title with How to play focused.
- **Shared look**: the world map, world pages and How to play use `.home-card` (a soft gradient, a line under the heading, a quick fade-in), pill-shaped Back buttons, and tiles with a gold glow on focus. Gold `.primary` buttons have a slight gradient everywhere in the game, at the same size as before.
- **World map** (`showWorlds`): a tile per world with its number, name, how many levels, Team / Versus / Team + versus / Boss (every 10th world is a boss world), and a dot per level that fills once it's played. A tile shows its first level's preview standing still and plays all its levels' previews in turn while focused or hovered. While on the title, the game screen behind the menu takes the focused world's colours.
- **World map pages** (Gavyn, Nov 2026; 20 a page at first, cut to 10 a page on 2026-11-12): 10 worlds a page (`WORLDS_PER_PAGE`), 2 rows of 5. Every page ends on its boss world (10, 20, 30...), so the boss always sits in the bottom-right corner. The 5 columns are fixed (a container query on `.wpager`, css/style.css): on a small screen such as a phone held sideways the tiles shrink (smaller name, the level count hidden, dots kept) instead of wrapping, and only a box under 400 px wide falls back to 5 rows of 2, never a ragged 3 or 4 across. A ▶ "More" arrow on the right goes to the next 10 and a ◀ "Back" arrow on the left to the one before; page dots and "Worlds 21 to 25" under the grid. Pages are counted from `liveWorlds()`, so new worlds land on the next page by themselves (checked with 47 pretend worlds). The arrows are sticky, so they stay on screen while a tall page scrolls. Keyboard and controller reach them by moving right (or left) past the last tile, then Enter / A; focus stays on the arrow so you can keep paging, or lands on the page's first world at the last page. Flipping redraws only the grid (`showWorldPage`), so quick taps all count. The map opens on the page of the world played last, and Back from a world's page returns to that world's page. Tests: `showWorlds(w)` (a global) opens the map on world `w`'s page; a tile for a world on another page is not in the page until you flip to it, so older checks that look for `.wtile[data-w=N]` with N >= 20 (tools/w21-w24) need that first (now any N >= 10). `node tools/world-map/pages.js` checks 2 rows of 5, bosses at the end of each page, and paging with keyboard, a stubbed controller and touch at 1280x800, 844x390 and 390x844.
- **World page** (`showWorld`): the world's blurb, "Play World N from N-1" (plays the whole world in order), ◀ ▶ buttons to the next and previous worlds, and a card per level with its moving preview, code, name, Team/Versus tag, best time or strokes if any, a ✓ once played, and a one-line blurb. In 1-player mode one line says who the computer plays and how.
- **From the pause card**: "Choose a level" opens the current world's page; Back from the world map says "Back to the game" and returns to the pause card.
- **Controls**: keyboard arrows or W A S D move between buttons on these screens (cards marked `data-nav`), Enter or Space picks, Esc goes back (a `data-back` button). Controller: stick or d-pad, A picks, B goes back. Touch: tap. Other cards (results, pause) keep their old keys.
- "Played" is kept in the browser per level name (`store` key `done:<name>`), set when a results card shows (a lost Robo Pong doesn't count), plus any saved best time or golf score.
- **Previews**: `definePreview('<level name>', { blurb, loop, still, draw(v, t) })` in `js/previews/worldN.js`. `draw` paints a 320x180 scene at time `t` with the kit `v` (documented at the top of `preview-core.js`): world-themed scenery, `v.hero(0|1, x, y, {face, sq, mood, s})` for Pip and Bo in their game look, bubbles, confetti and easing helpers. A preview is a short looping cartoon (3-7 s) that shows the level's twist with a happy payoff (team) or a winner (versus); `still` is the moment shown as a thumbnail and to people who prefer reduced motion; `blurb` is one plain sentence (80 characters or fewer). Rules: a pure function of `t` (no `Math.random`, no state), never touches `G`, `C` or the game canvas, every top-level helper name prefixed with `pw<world>` (all scripts share one scope), under 3 ms a frame, Pip orange and Bo blue, family-friendly. A preview that throws falls back to the plain scene and logs a warning once.
- **Checking previews**: `node tools/preview-shots.js <world|all> [--frames=8] [--size=1] [--times=0,4.9]` writes a contact sheet per level to `build/previews/` (the still frame, then frames across the loop) and prints errors and ms per frame.
- Previews draw at about 30 frames a second and only while on screen; the loop stops when the menu closes.


### Computer partner (1-player mode)
- Settings: the title and pause cards have Players (2 players / 1 player + computer), You play (Pip / Bo) and Opponent (Easy / Normal / Hard, versus levels only). Saved in the browser (`store` key `cpu`). `G.cpuOn = [pip, bo]` says who the computer plays.
- In 1-player mode the person can use either player's keys, the first controller is theirs, the computer's touch pad and legend line are hidden, and the computer's name tag reads "Bo · CPU". It talks in short speech bubbles ("Hop on my head!") only in 1-player mode.
- The computer presses buttons like a person: each physics step `cpuTick` asks the level's brain for `{ left, right, jump, down, up, act }` and turns it into `p.input` / `p.pendingJump` / `p.pendingAct`. Levels never need to know who is playing. It never cheats with level state, except where noted in its brain file.
- It can't finish a team level alone: if the person presses nothing for 30 s (`CPU.idleStop`), it waits too.
- Files: `js/cpu/cpu-core.js` (settings, brain registry, `cpuTick`, helpers, bubbles; its header explains the brain API), `js/cpu/cpu-nav.js` (`cpuNav`: path finding over the tile map with walk, drop and jump moves, used by platform levels; opts `pads` and `portals` add bounce-pad throws and portal hops, as 4-4 uses), and `js/cpu/levels/` with one brain file per level file (World 1 in `world1.js`). Script tags go after the level files and before `js/input.js`.
- Brain key: the level's `def.mode`; older levels use `level:<name>` (World 1), `build`, `pong`, `crown`, `golf`. A level with no brain falls back to `follow` (stay near the partner), so a new level works but plays badly until it gets a brain.
- Skill: `info.skill` is 0.35 / 0.7 / 1. The setting is called Opponent and only applies to versus levels: in team levels `cpuSkill` always gives 1, so the teammate always plays its best (Gavyn, Nov 2026). Versus brains use it for reaction time, aim and mistakes. Bot tests can still force a skill with `--skills`.
- World 1 approach: the brain works out who does which job (holder or crosser, carrier or rider) from where the partner stands, waits for a person who is slow, and asks for help in a bubble.
- Every brain was written, then reviewed by a second agent that tried to break it with scripted "people" (who take the computer's job first, wait, wander, go the wrong way) and fixed what it found. Each brain file's header explains its plan.
- Speech bubbles: short, only in 1-player mode, never while the intro card is up or while the person is away, and a bubble that has been up 4 s rests for 5 s. Bigger on phones. Levels whose players are hidden (golf ball, plinko, car) set `bubbleHidden`. Hints, tips and pills that would tell the person to press keys for the computer say what the computer does instead (`cpuSolo() && cpuOn(id)` checks in the level files); intro and pause tips that name the computer's character get "You play Pip. The computer does Bo's part."
- Versus balance (computer vs computer, many seeds): no side bias, Hard beats Easy clearly, Easy is beatable by a kid-like scripted player. Hard reacts in about 0.12-0.15 s. Luck-heavy levels (8-2 Roulette) give Hard a smaller edge. Real playtests may still show tuning is needed.
- Known weak spots: 6-4 Sumo: a player who just keeps hopping beats Hard about 30% of the time; 9-4 Krakatoa: getting past the partner runs on rules of thumb, so unusual partner habits may still find a stall.
- On phones the computer's touch pad is hidden and only one side gutter is kept, so the game screen is centred. A controller's View button swaps which character the person plays.

### Testing
- `window.__pipbo` exposes the game state and functions (`G`, `LEVELS`, `MODES`, `loadLevel`, `step`, `matchRoundEnd`, `pause`, and more) for bot tests.
- Bot tests run in headless Chromium with Playwright: set `G.manual = true`, write each player's input, and call `step(DT)`. Set `G.introT = 0` to skip the intro card. Controller tests stub `navigator.getGamepads`.
- `node tools/cpu-test.js [levels|all] --who=pair|noisy|idle|solo [--clumsy=1] [--skills=easy,hard] [--seeds=1,2,3]` plays levels with the computer at the controls. `pair`: computer plays both (team levels must clear). `noisy`: one side is made clumsy like a distracted person (pauses, wanders, random presses) and the level must still clear. `idle`: the person presses nothing and a team level must not clear. `--skills=easy,hard` checks that Hard beats Easy in versus levels.
- `node tools/physics-check.js [levels|all|w18] --who=cpu|mash|mix [--seeds=1,2] [--max=240] [--allwalls] [--deep]` is the movement check (Nov 2026 review of Worlds 1-28). It plays levels with the computer, with random button mashing, or a mix, and watches every physics step: NaN positions or level state, a player inside solid tiles, lost off screen, falling forever, faster than anything should go, a position that flips direction every step (shake), and 8 s of pressing buttons without moving (stuck). `stuck`, `offscreen` and `wall?` need a look before they count: thrower, aimer and driver jobs sit still on purpose, and levels with their own coordinates park players off screen. The review found one bug this way (18-2: ▲ plus sideways at the foot of the rope ladder flipped between climbing and walking every tick, a 4 px shake) and nothing in the shared movement code. Levels that change `PHYS` for a moment (15-2, 17-3, 19-1, 20-1) all put it back in a `finally`.
- A second, line-by-line pass (one reviewer per world, each finding reproduced in the browser) started with Worlds 1-4 and 6. It found and fixed: 1-4 Chain room (a chain that a wall had stretched pulled a player through the tall wall when both stood, and snapped the holder back up to 95 px; `limitLength` now pulls in small pieces with `pullX`, and a held chain that is over length holds the walker back); 2-1/2-2 (a block could land within half a pixel of the climber, who could then stand in mid-air against it and climb a sheer wall; `pieceFits` and `towerPush` now use the same 0.01 px inset as the movement code); 4-1 Springs (a jump pressed just after a spring replaced the launch); 6-3 Train Tracks (a held diagonal at a tree, hut or bank corner froze the walker: the corner assist no longer pushes against the way they steer); 13-1 (the screen edges now stop the pusher, Gavyn's call).
- Later from the same pass (Gavyn's calls): rising through a `=` plank used to count as standing on it for one tick, which allowed a second jump there (198 px instead of 106 px in 4-1 and 4-4). `probe()` now only counts a plank as ground when not rising (`p.vy >= 0`), and `navSim` in `js/cpu/cpu-nav.js` has the same rule (keep the two in step). That change showed two computer-partner weak spots, both fixed: the 28-2 torch-carrier went for a brazier behind a shut gate, and two computer players that wanted the same take-off cell pushed against each other for good (cpuNav now jumps from where it stands after 0.4 s of that). 2-1 Brick by Brick: a climber in a 1-wide hole 4 blocks deep cannot get out; Gavyn says that works as intended.
- Worlds 5, 7 and 8 had the line-by-line pass next. Fixed: 7-4 Bumper Cars Tyre Wall (a ram's separation could push a car through the tyres: the fence check now runs again after the bumps); 7-4 edge teeter (holding out over the moat flipped drive/teeter about 20 times a second, with a Phew and an Edge save each time: a braked teeter now stays a teeter while the stick still points out); 5-3 Ice Slide (a jump pressed during a slide fired as a hop on the snow afterwards); 8-1 Plinko Drop (a power kick pressed on the tick the chip touched the paddle let the chip fall through).
- Worlds 9-12 had the line-by-line pass next. Fixed: 9-2 cable car (a rider in mid-hop as the car docked flew out of the open far wall, the car never parked, the crossing never counted and the gate stayed shut: a rider who is aboard but in the air now parks it too); 9-2 crates (two crates on one plank can overlap, and a boing off one snapped the player 23 px sideways off the other); 9-4 Cliff Path (the fan's gust reached a leader on the high path through the rock: the gust now needs the leader on the gap's own level); 10-1 Hot Floor Climb (Super steps put an extra cell on the holder's own respawn spot, which boxed them in under their steps: `gc3Super` leaves out an extra cell that overlaps a respawn spot); 11-2 Tandem Velodrome (no draft behind a bike a lap ahead or behind, and such a bike did not ride round the tandem: both gaps are wrapped to the lap now); 12-1 Curling (skating into the hack zone from above or below snapped the sweeper up to 84 px sideways: it is pushed out of the nearest side now); 12-4 Ice Hockey (a skater coming back over the goal line beside a post was snapped 11 px sideways: held at the line now; a body could push a loose puck through the boards for a few ticks).
- Worlds 13-16 had the line-by-line pass next. Fixed: 15-3 Moon Buggy Crew (a boulder dropped on the buggy's nose at a crater edge rolled into the crater as a track boulder: uncounted, later buried out of the claw's reach and in the buggy's way for good, a soft lock; it now plops into the crater and counts. Reversing is also stopped 160 px behind the start); 14-3 Tar Pit Pull (SHLORP with the puller's centre over the edge landed the rescued player on the pit floor under the tar, and their safe spot ended inside rock: the landing spot is never in a tar column now, and the pit floor is not a bank); 14-4 Raptor Race (a raptor carried over a fork line by the camera's left edge or a bump missed the crossing and stayed on the creek/treetop level: the fork checks remember where they left off, `r.chk`); 15-1 Tethered Spacewalk (a tether stretched past its limit by two moving handholds snapped the floater up to 130 px on the first reel tap: it comes in at reel speed now; reeling in no longer drags the floater into a rock); 16-1 Conveyor Belt Chaos (the lift pushed a sorter with part of the body under the shelf up into it, and carried a sorter standing on the belt end down through the belt); 13-2 Graveyard Bone Hunt (jump still held at the top of the ladder plus a direction shook the player at the lip: a held jump no longer re-grabs the ladder after topping out); 13-1 (after a poof back to the bale the screen-edge rule put the pusher on the far side of the pumpkin for a moment); 13-4 Broomstick Race (flying low into a wall or chimney popped the broom up 50 px in one step: it slides up now).
- Worlds 17-20 had the line-by-line pass next. Fixed: 19-1 Train Heist Hop (a ducked player walked under the front wall on the engine's lower boiler; standing up threw them 968 px above the screen or off the train: the wall reaches down to the boiler now); 17-1 Temple Doors (pulling the idol snapped the puller through it: on a pull the cell the puller stands in only turns to stone on arrival, and the nudge ignores the idol's own cells; go-back and Splash spots move beside an idol pushed onto them, `templeSpotNow`); 17-3 Waterfall Climb (after climbing down a vine each jump tap climbed the bare wall one tile: the climber steps clear of the face; a partner in mid-jump no longer counts as ground, which gave a double jump higher than a boost; a rider on the partner's head is carried by the log too); 17-2 (the Rocky Rapids boulder that sat on the first flag's respawn spot moved 120 px on); 18-4 Plank Duel (the rocking plank or a gust slid a duellist standing exactly at their end off with no bonk; a "Phew!" step back with the other duellist close restarted the teeter every tick: the step back moves the other duellist); 18-1 Runaway Ship (the hop off the rope ladder never happened because the held jump grabbed it again: 0.3 s no-grab; jump + down in the nest flipped nest/ladder); 18-2 Grapple & Zip (a hop frozen for a whole ladder climb or zip; a zipper who fell past the deck was snapped up onto it); 19-3 Gold Mine Cart (lever presses during the mule pull-back were dropped); 20-1 Volcano Dragon (a player over the lava lake when the levers phase ended fell through and was killed: the leave cutscene keeps the lava rescue; letting go of a grip wall kept coyote time for a mid-air jump).
- Worlds 21-24 had the line-by-line pass next. Fixed: 21-3 Cloud Bridge Builder (the leash undid a Boing come-back when the partner was over 700 px away: the player reappeared in mid-air where they fell and Boinged again for ever; a player just back from a Boing is left out of the leash that tick. Also a jump just after the rainbow spring replaced the launch, and a jump on the tick of an auto-step was swallowed); 22-2 Treasure Diver Rope (reeling the diver up under a ledge threw them over 100 px sideways through it: after the rope pull the up/down pass runs first); 22-3 Shipwreck Lights (the leash could leave a swimmer 1 px inside a wall corner, and the next step threw them across the wall); 22-1 Submarine Duo (a let-go boulder was not solid while sinking, then popped the sub up to 50 px on landing); 23-2 Runaway Pigs (the bale rolled over the herder, whose next step threw them across the map; the gold hay locked onto a player in the gap; the bale squeezed a pig through a fence as a wrong turn: `hayFree` now treats the herder and a squeezed pig as in the way, and both locks nudge people out with `hayNudge`); 24-4 Pillow Fort Battle (a player could wall themselves in behind their own shield: the Boing rescue looks for the nearest wall on each side, so pits wider than 1 column count); 24-2 Jack-in-the-Box Launch (`st.boxU` was never reset, so from the second haul on the box jumped to the next shelf for a tick and slid back). Looked at and left: 24-1 a train coasting over a switch point during "Taking a break" (the checker could not reproduce it).
- Worlds 25-28 had the line-by-line pass last (16 fixes; with this every level of Worlds 1-28 has been read line by line). 25-1: an under-ground digger now starts at most 2 deep, so the shaft it leaves in a zombie lane is 3 deep and a jump gets out (4 deep was a soft lock); `canPlace` and the front column in `zfTarget` use the 0.01 px inset of moveX/moveY (a block could go in overlapping a body by up to 1 px: wall climb by jump taps, sideways snap when building under your feet); the walls either side of a pad cannot both be taller than 3 (a boxed-in respawn pad). 26-4: a topple in mid-hop ends the hop. 26-3: the net truck stops at the end of the grounds (except the drive-off). 27-4: landing on the other robot's head slides you off at 150 px/s at most (it was +60 px/s every step, about 750 px/s, a ring-out). 27-1: the gold plank is solid (`solids()` and `floorAt`), and the first gap is 104 px (90 was narrower than the mech, so it could not be fallen into). 27-2: a fall jam only stops the line once the far side of the gap is inside the runner's right limit. 27-3: the rider ignores the operator's body in the cab; a crate landing on a riding rider lifts them on top; a lowered magnet driven at a taller column waits and winds itself up (no 180 px snap). 28-1: the squish shove uses `pyrMoveX` (the plain x set pushed a player into a shut door or rubble and `pyrMoveY` then lifted them onto the roof: the run could be finished with no lever or smash); the leash leaves a player alone on the tick they pop back from a Whoops (it used to put them back over the gap). 28-3: a stone cannot be pushed or pulled onto the reset tile (a heavy stone there locked puzzle 3).
- `node tools/controls/check.js` checks the ready card and device detection: keyboard, Xbox + PlayStation, an unknown controller, 1-player mode and a phone each ready up their own way, and every level opens on its card, starts and plays for a few seconds without an error. `node tools/controls/shots.js` saves screenshots of the card on each device to `build/controls-shots/`.
- Test every new level with a keyboard, a controller and touch, on desktop and phone sizes, and play it through to the results card.
- For refactors: `/mnt/project-files/pip-and-bo/refactor-tools/` (in the Claude project files) has a deterministic old-vs-new play-through (`harness.js` + `cmp.js`, run with `runall.sh`): fake clock, seeded random, every level, all three input types, comparing canvas, game state, menus, audio and saves.

### Publishing
- The main home is GitHub Pages at https://gavyn-nygren.github.io/2d-VideoGame/. `.github/workflows/pages.yml` deploys it on every push to `main` (a merged PR counts), so there is nothing to publish by hand and no size limit. It copies `css/` and `js/` as they are and puts a doctype and phone viewport on top of `index.html` (the repo's `index.html` stays a page body for the artifact). Check a deploy under the repo's Actions tab ("Deploy game to GitHub Pages"); it can also be run by hand from there. One-time setup: the repo must be public (or the account on GitHub Pro) and Settings > Pages > Source set to "GitHub Actions". Saves live per website, so progress from the artifact demo does not carry over.
- The demo is a multi-file claude.ai Artifact at https://claude.ai/artifact/85NPz4hhcdi429juHSXUx7: the page is the repo's `index.html` and every css/js file is published next to it under its own path (`files`). To update it, read the page and list its files first, then publish `index.html` with the changed files. Always republish to that same link. (Older single-file demos, which got too big to update: 3KFgRXoujADJ9PMR1rTErY, 5jJ7SJw7gwSttznxFP56TG.)
- `node tools/bundle.js` (needs only Node) writes `build/pip-and-bo.html`, one self-contained file that is not committed (handy for sharing offline; the demo no longer uses it). Like `index.html`, it is the page body without the outer html/head/body tags, which the publisher adds.
- Then push to `main` here and update the README's world list and this file.

## Open questions for Gavyn
- 29-1: Gold needs 3:50 or less with at most 3 whoops; a good scripted person with the computer takes about 2:45 and a slow kid about 4:05 (Silver). Should Gold be tighter, say 3:20, so it feels earned?
- 29-1: The new edge catch saves near-miss leaps and jumps ('Phew!', counted); should it also cost a little, for example counting toward the medal, or stay a free close call?
- 29-1: A runner now stops at the edge of a wide gap instead of running off; do you want that safety, or should running off stay possible as a soft fail?
- 29-1: A cat dozes for 1.8 s after a bump so nobody gets stuck behind it; is that kind enough, or should cats only bump once each?
- 29-1: Is four or five hook gaps per stretch (17 in all) the right length, or would three or four per stretch feel snappier?
- 29-2: chases are now about 2 s (about 0.7 s of running plus a 1.5 s 'Shh!' hide while the guards look around). Do you want longer chases, for example a wider 'they saw you here' radius than 130 px or a longer hide?
- 29-2: in a chase only the sneaker runs (sprint, hop, slide) while the thrower bonks guards from the wall. Is that OK, or should the thrower have to run too?
- 29-2: the gold owl (after 45 s stuck at one post) can lure the guards for a thrower who isn't throwing. Is 45 s right, or should the owl only help after the thrower has tossed a few pebbles?
- 29-2: Gold is 4:30 or under, spotted at most 2 and never caught. A 1-second-slow kid with the computer partner still gets Gold (4:11); should Gold be tighter, for example 4:00?
- 29-2: a button-masher sneaker is now caught over and over (about 75 times in 15 minutes) and never finishes. Should there be a third gold help for repeated catches at one post?
- 29-3: Gold help now lasts 15 s and can be earned again after 3 more misses (a masher gets 8-10 gold helps in a ride). Is 15 s right, or should it be shorter or longer?
- 29-3: Should the star warning (1.05 s in stretch 1 down to 0.85 s in stretch 4) be longer for younger players? The ~1 s-slow 'kid' script still mostly got Silver.
- 29-3: The course sets the time (about 3:20 for any decent pair), so the 3:40 Gold time limit rarely matters. Should Gold also need a share of Greats (stars bonked back) to reward sword timing?
- 29-3: The soft brake has almost no use (it only slows about a third and nothing needs it). Should it give something, like a wider swing window while braking, or be dropped?
- 29-4: Two strong players who dodge almost everything often play to the 45 s clock (time-up goes to fewer bonks, then more deflects); should rounds be shorter (35 s) or should stars speed up as the clock runs down?
- 29-4: Should the pouch stop refilling while ducking (new rule, it is what stops duck-camping)? Or would you rather ducking cost something else, such as slower hops?
- 29-4: Normal now goes about 50/50 with an everyday dodger but loses to someone who parries well (about 90% for the person); should Normal parry more, or is learning to parry the right way to beat it?
- 29-4: A press of the action when a star is under 0.42 s away always swings and never throws; is that the feel you want, or should swinging be a separate input?
- 29-4: Gold help (a wider deflect window, then a bigger pouch) also goes to the computer when it is behind a person; keep it for both sides, or people only?
- 29-4: Gavyn's idea of a short charge for a faster star is not built (depth comes from the pouch, lanes, stepping closer, the lantern and rallies); want a charged throw added?
- 30-1 The Time Thief: the whole level is about 24 minutes for a very good pair and 30-45 for most people (checkpoints save every era). Is that the size you wanted, or should some eras be shorter?
- 30-1 Portal Race Home is best of 3, so a 2-0 win never shows race 3 (rooftops, Kitty Hawk, the Time Tunnel). Always run all 3 races?
- 30-1 The End of Time: the tower has 6 climbs and on the last two the lever kicks (let go when it flashes red, or lose your grip and shake your friend off). Right amount of mean?
- 30-1 Leonardo's Wings: should collecting all 111 pages count toward the trophy, or stay a bragging line?
- 30-1 The Fall of Troy: the horse coasts about 3 s after the roller lets go (heavy on purpose). Fun, or should it stop sooner?
- World 30 is called "Time Chase" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- 26-1 to 26-3: a computer pair finishes each in 1.5-3 minutes; people will be slower. Are these lengths right, or should acts, stretches or tours be longer?
- 26-1 Trapeze Catch: is timing the let-go hard for young kids? Gold help widens the catch; should it also slow the swing?
- 26-4 Stilt Race: is the lean balance too hard on Easy for little kids?
- 28-4 Camel Race: rounds are about 35-45 s. Longer or shorter?
- 23-1 Tractor & Trailer: You can finish without delivering much: a pair that holds ▶ the whole way reaches the Big Barn in 110 to 130 s with 0 to 24 of 84 items and still clears with a Bronze. That fits the soft-fail rule. Should finishing need a minimum load, or should racing past the tossers make them wait?
- 23-1 Tractor & Trailer: The new gold engine lets a kid who only mashes, holds ◀ or only honks get to the barn. With the computer catching, that kid gets Silver in about 6 minutes (50 to 75% delivered). Should using the gold engine cap the medal at Bronze?
- 23-1 Tractor & Trailer: The Gold line (3:45 with 70%) splits our kid-pair runs between Gold and Silver (222 to 239 s). Should it go up to about 4:00 so steady young pairs get Gold more often?
- 23-1 Tractor & Trailer: Several pop words ('Hay!', 'Right there!', 'Nice and slow!', 'Hold tight!', 'Baa! Okay!') can stack up over the wagon or a roadside sign for a moment. They are readable but busy, most of all on a phone.
- 23-1 Tractor & Trailer: The wobble meter is a small dark bar with two little bales on top, at the back of the wagon. Is it clear enough for a 6-year-old, or should it say 'wobble'?
- 23-2 Runaway Pigs: A computer pair finishes in about 2:18, faster than the 3-4 min asked for. People land at 3-6 min. Make the chases longer or the pigs more slippery, or is this fine?
- 23-2 Runaway Pigs: A slow kid with the computer takes about 6 min and slow-kid pairs 8-10 min, even with the new gold gate after 25 s and the gold ribbon after 40 s. Should help come sooner (for example 15-20 s), or is that too much for grown-ups?
- 23-2 Runaway Pigs: Gold gates are now permanent (an opened gold gate stays open, a gold lever stays on the right way), so a kid mashing the button can't undo them. Fine, or should a gold gate go back to normal after the herd passes?
- 23-2 Runaway Pigs: Gold ribbons now count as one gold help a chase, however many pigs get one (they were counted per pig, which pushed kid pairs to Bronze). Is that the right way to count them?
- 23-2 Runaway Pigs: Gold needs at most 1 gold help, so one gold arrow plus one gold gate in a whole level means Silver. Too strict?
- 23-2 Runaway Pigs: In 2-player, a gatekeeper standing still by a gate counts as playing for 90 s (for gold help). In 1-player the person must press something every 10 s for help to come, and the computer herder waits after 90 s with no presses (30 s in other levels). Are these timings right?
- 23-3 Hot Dog Stand Rush: Slow-kid pairs (both players about 1 s slow at every step) make only about 1-2 happy fairgoers a shift and get Bronze. Nobody walks off; the shift just ends with people still waiting. Should the goals drop from 4/5/4/6 to about 3/4/3/5, or should the shifts be longer for them?
- 23-3 Hot Dog Stand Rush: In 1-player mode, the stand now waits for a person who has had a job for 30 s and done none of it. Is 30 s right, or would 20 s feel better?
- 23-3 Hot Dog Stand Rush: Orders get one hot dog bigger once 2 fairgoers wait beyond the window. Slow pairs make the line long, so they get the bigger orders most. Should this wait until 3 are in line?
- 23-3 Hot Dog Stand Rush: Golden now lasts 8.5 s (12 s with the gold timer). Is that generous enough for young cooks, or too easy for older ones?
- 23-3 Hot Dog Stand Rush: The order tickets and the customers' bubbles are still small on a phone at 844x390 (the ticket hot dogs are about 20 px). Are the topping colours readable enough there?
- 23-4 Pie-Eating Contest: 23-4 Pie-Eating Contest: a GULP! now gives 2 bonus bites (it was 1), so gulping on the clap clearly beats ignoring the claps at the same biting speed. Even so, a fast player who ignores the claps still splits about evenly with a slower calm kid who keeps to the beat. Should timing count for more still, for example with a bigger GULP! bonus or a harsher Glug?
- 23-4 Pie-Eating Contest: 23-4: Easy was made gentler (slower bites, forgets 1 clap in 4). Rounds against Easy now last about 42 s, and a turbo button on both buttons takes about 4 rounds in 10 from it (button mashers still lose). Is that the right place for little kids?
- 23-4 Pie-Eating Contest: 23-4: Hard is unchanged. A very good grown-up takes about a third of the rounds and a good one takes none. Is that right for Hard?
- 23-4 Pie-Eating Contest: 23-4: the Glug's air is softer now (32%, 15% with empty cheeks, was 40/20%), so a kid who presses ▲ at random gets fewer hiccups. Keep it, or soften it more?
- 20-1 Volcano Dragon: Hopping Pillars is the shortest phase (a good pair about 1 minute) and the one where kids get toasted most. Softer guard flames, or longer?
- 20-1: Gong Golf has 2 holes (about 1-1.5 minutes for people). Add a 3rd hole?
- 20-1: Surf Race on Easy: a very young kid who only holds ▶ and mashes loses to Easy. Make Easy softer still?
- 20-1: dropping the climb's rope ladder is a 0.55 s hold at the hook (a plain press let a masher clear the climb). Keep the hold?
- 21-1 Hot Air Balloon Climb: Gold was loosened to ≤ 4:30, ≤ 6 bumps and 50% of the 64 stars (Nov 2026). Holding the burner about 2 s makes it sputter: too strict? Help when stuck under one spot comes after 25 s; sooner?
- 21-2 Kite Tandem: Gold now needs 40% of the stars (a kid pair gets about 35 of 79). Should the rider's lean drift back to the middle when let go?
- 21-3 Cloud Bridge Builder: in the sunny stretch clouds last only about 3.3 s. Too quick for little kids? The cloud lift comes down when you walk into its side: easy enough to find?
- 21-4 Paper Plane Race: the catch-up help is strong (the trailer is pulled along and gets a tailwind), so races end close. Too much for grown-ups? Keep the swinging throw meter and 3 flaps a round?
- World 22 is called "Coral Deep" (Claude's suggestion; Gavyn chose the underwater theme). Keep it or rename it?
- 22-1 Submarine Duo: slow-kid pairs take 6-7.5 min, longer than other team levels, mostly at the log and boulder jobs. Trim a pearl group or one blocker per stretch? Carrying the boulder forward through the pinch also clears the way: welcome, or only the sandy hole? The steerer's action only toots the horn (moves pufferfish, scatters fish): enough to do? The clock stops during "Taking a break" (12 s with nobody pressing): fine for a time medal?
- 22-2 Treasure Diver Rope: eels now reach about 50 px further so they cross the paths ("dodging sleepy eels"); careful players still get 0-2 frights. Right amount? A good pair takes about 2:55: should it be closer to 4 minutes? A boat partner who never pumps clears with gold help in about 5 min: generous enough for small kids? The tight rope can be drawn crossing a ledge (looks only).
- 22-3 Shipwreck Lights: Treasure and keys are grabbed by swimming into them (levers, wheels and the chest use the action): keep? Keep the faint dashed outline where a lever, wheel or key was seen? Gold at 3:45, ≤ 5 bonks, 60% treasure: tune after kid playtests (best pair 2:33).
- 22-4 Dolphin Race: a very young kid pressing at random (or holding ▲) always loses to Easy, even loses to a player who presses nothing, because each bump halves your speed. Soften bumps, or make Easy weaker still? Catch-up help is strong (pulled along plus a gold current, as in 21-4), so Normal sometimes beats Hard: fine for family play? Leaps are unlimited (1.6 s refill, a big landing costs 4%): keep, or cap them per round like the paper plane's 3 flaps?
- 24-1 Wind-Up Train Ride: a good pair takes about 3 1/4 minutes and kid pairs about 3 1/2 (45-55 s a stretch). Right length, or trim each course about 10% to feel snappier?
- 24-1: Gold is ≤ 4:00, ≤ 4 clunks and half the stars. Prompt-following kid pairs just make Gold (3-4 clunks), slower kids get Silver. Allow a couple more clunks?
- 24-1: a kid who only mashes the wind button crawls until the gold key comes after 20 s. Too strict, or is it good that the beat ring teaches the rhythm? A bouncing toy (ball, yo-yo, metronome) naps after 15 s of waiting: sooner for little kids?
- 24-1: in 1-player mode the computer winder waits at a station until the person helps the passenger on. Should it give up after a while and roll on (passenger missed)? Kid pairs get 3-5 gold helps a ride ("Gold help: N times. Next time, try it without!"): friendly enough, or should help come later for grown-ups?
- 24-2 Jack-in-the-Box Launch: short (computer pair about 1:50, people probably 2.5-3.5 min). Add a third launch per shelf? Is one beat every 0.55 s right for kids, or slow down the first shelf?
- 24-2: two button-mashers reach the top in about 6 min (Bronze) thanks to gold help, and a masher with the computer gets Silver (about 2.5 min). Should help need more careful presses, or Silver need some on-beat notes (say 40% Perfect)? Is Gold (≤ 3:45, ≤ 3 boings, 55% stars, no help) the right bar? Keep the bouncy ribbon as a helper?
- 24-3 Slinky Stairs: in 1-player mode the shelf signs say what the computer does ("Bo (computer) points the way"). OK, or hide those signs? Is the steerer's lean (about ±40 px over a flop) still useful, or too weak?
- 24-3: in 1-player the computer's two steering flights alone give 32 perfects (Gold needs 30), so Gold could need 36, or count only the person's perfects. A masher with the computer finishes in 3:20-4:30 (Silver/Bronze); two mashers reach only flight 3-4 in 10 min: OK for small kids? Always show the green band? Are 7-second pillows too quick? Should the steerer have to point before every flop?
- 24-4 Pillow Fort Battle: at the 75 s bell the bigger fort now wins by even one block (same size: the steadier teddy). OK, or should close forts replay? Kids nearly always play to the bell, so a best of 5 takes 6-7 min: shorter rounds (60 s) or teddies with 4 stars instead of 5?
- 24-4: Hard pairs finish rounds in about 33 s, Normal in about 49 s: right pace for grown-ups? A careful kid script never beats Normal: too strong for older kids? Does the full aim arc with the "Teddy!" label make hitting the teddy too easy? Is the toppled teddy's "x x" dizzy-eyes face in the preview soft enough?
- World 18 is called "Pirate Cove" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- 18-1 Runaway Ship takes a good pair about 3:40, the longest in World 18. Trim a hazard group per stretch (about 3:00)? Prompt-following kids get Gold; should Gold also need 4 of 6 big waves met? After 20 s at the cove a gold anchor lets a button-masher with the computer finish (Bronze), like 17-2.
- 18-1: a missed wave throws a deckhand who isn't holding on (▼) overboard; Dot the dolphin brings them back in 2.4 s. Keep it?
- 18-2 Grapple & Zip: 4 fleets take 2.5-4 minutes. Gold needs time, 65% of the coins and few misses, so most people with the computer get Silver. Is the capstan beat (0.3-1.15 s apart) too forgiving?
- 18-3 Tide Pool Dig: a coin a crab gets home is gone for good. Should stolen coins come back, or crabs only take loose coins? Gold (≤ 240 s, ≥ 85% coins) may be easy; tune after playtests.
- 18-4 Plank Duel: at time-up the round goes to more sword bonks, then plank kept (not "nearer the middle", which walking forward would win). Standing is high guard and holding ▼ is low guard, since ▲ is jump. Check that Normal isn't too hard for kids; feint-spamming ▼ beats Easy.
- World 19 is called "Tumbleweed Gulch" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- 19-1 Train Heist Hop: the thrower can't carry their own sack over a wide gap ("Let Bo grab it!"), and a sack left lying for 8 s goes to the partner. Clear enough for kids? Gold is ≤3:45 with ≤4 whoopses: too generous for two people?
- 19-2 Handcar Hustle: pushing at the same moment as your partner (within 0.11 s) jams the handle. Keep that? Is the curve's tip-then-Whoa grace right for young kids?
- 19-3 Gold Mine Cart: a wrong turn sends the cart back to a safe run-up spot, costing a few seconds. About 3.5 minutes for a good pair. Right length?
- 19-4 Quick-Draw Showdown: rounds take about 12 s because the shared Round card and 3-2-1 come before the paces. (Easy was slowed to about 0.55 s for young kids, Gavyn, Nov 2026.)
- World 17 is called "Jungle Jamboree" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- 17-2 River Rapids Canoe runs about 3.5 minutes for a good pair, the longest in World 17. A random button-masher with the computer can still clear it slowly (Bronze); making that impossible would also punish small kids.
- 17-4 Vine Swing: you can only let go on the forward swing, and Hard is close to perfect. Check that Easy feels fair for kids. With no swing clock, check the bigger swing (about 7 s of pumping to the top, about 80 m at best) doesn't make rounds drag.
- World 13 is called "Spooky Hollow" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- World 14 is called "Dino Valley" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- World 15 is called "Orbit Outpost" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- World 16 is called "Sugar Rush Factory" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- 16-1 Conveyor Belt Chaos: room 1 needs only one lever press from the operator (a warm-up). Give the lever worker more to do there?
- 16-2 Gumball Machine Puzzle: in machines 2-4 the hints only show after 8 s of waiting or a wrong cup, so the puzzle is left to the players. Always show them, as in machine 1?
- 16-3 Chocolate River Raft runs about 3.5-4.5 minutes and most prompt-following crews get Gold. Trim a stretch or tighten Gold after playtests? The hook moves fast (380 px/s): check it isn't twitchy on a stick.
- 16-4 Sprinkle Catch: a bump is a nudge (the bumped player gets 1 s to scoop the spilled candy back and can't be bumped again for 2.5 s), and Jelly Floor bounces settle. Is that the right feel?
- 15-4 Planet Hop Race: running round a planet is relative to your own up, but in the air ◀ ▶ and the jet puff follow the screen (changed in review because it saved far more jumps). Check it feels right with kids.
- 14-2 Pterodactyl Express runs about 4.5 minutes for a good pair (longest in World 14) and 14-3 Tar Pit Pull about 1-3 minutes. Shorten or lengthen after playtests?
- World 9 is called "Great Escapes" (Claude's pick). Keep it or rename it?
- Crown Keep (2-4) is one 90-second game, not best of 3/5/7. It was built before that rule. Should it become a best-of match?
- 27-1 Mech Suit Duo: the arms pilot has three arm heights and has to match the wall's glowing spot. Is that too much to read for a 6-year-old, or should a punch at any height chip the wall (just slower)?
- 27-2 Runaway Assembly Line: there's no clock; the medal counts bumps and gears. Should finishing time count too?
- 27-3 Magnet Crane Crew: a clumsy partner takes 4-6 minutes. Should the gold guide come sooner than 30 s?
- 27-4 Robot Rumble: a rumble often ends in 3-8 s once both robots charge. Should shoves be weaker so rounds last longer?
